# 2606.26919v1_The_parental_parsimony_problem_on_binary__tree-child_phylogenetic_networks

## Page 1


The parental parsimony problem on binary, tree-child phylogenetic
networks
Martin Frohna,∗,1
aDepartment of Advanced Computing Sciences, Maastricht University, Paul Henri Spaaklaan 1, 6229 EN Maastricht, Netherlands.
A R T I C L E I N F O
Keywords:
Combinatorial optimization; integer
programming; approximation algo-
rithms; phylogenetics;
A B S T R A C T
Phylogenetic reconstruction is one of the major challenges in computational biology. Among
existing reconstruction methods for phylogenetic networks, an important subtask emerges in
extending a leaf-labelling on a phylogenetic network to determine a most parsimonious tree
inside the network. There exist different variants of this subtask depending on the biological
model assumptions for which distinct evolutionary phenomena are captured by the network. In
this article we assume that next to hybridization or recombination events, also allopolyploidy
or incomplete lineage sorting are present. Then, finding the most parsimonious tree inside
the network is called the parental parsimony score problem (PPS), a NP-hard combinatorial
optimization problem. We provide the first constant-factor approximation for the PPS on arbitrary
but fixed leaf labels and a class of networks on which the PPS remains NP-hard, namely
binary, semi-simplex, tree-child phylogenetic networks. Furthermore, we introduce a novel exact
solution algorithm for the PPS on binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks and analyze its
performance on simulated data.
1. Introduction
Molecular phylogenetic reconstruction methods infer evolutionary histories from molecular data [3]. Consider a
set Γ = {𝑥1, 𝑥2, … , 𝑥𝑛} of 𝑛≥3 distinct aligned molecular sequences (such as DNA, RNA, codon sequences or
whole genomes), called taxa. For example, 𝑥∈Γ can take values over the set {𝐴, 𝐶, 𝐺, 𝑇} for DNA sequences or 𝑥is
encoded by one of the 20 amino acids for protein sequences. Then, we can represent the pattern of diversification events
throughout evolutionary history that give rise to the set of taxa Γ by an ordered triplet (𝑇, 𝜙, 𝑤), called a phylogenetic
tree of Γ, such that 𝑇= (𝑉, 𝐸) is a rooted tree having 𝑛leaves, 𝜙is a bijection between the leaves of 𝑇and the taxa
in Γ, and 𝑤is a vector of non-negative weights associated to the edges of 𝑇. The latter vector 𝑤represents the quantity
of evolutionary change along a given edge. We make our study independent of biological assumptions on the set of
taxa by choosing an integer 𝑝≥1 such that all taxa in Γ take values over an alphabet 𝑆𝑝= {0, 1, … , 𝑝}. In particular,
we assume that our molecular data is given by a set of functions 𝐶∶Γ →𝑆𝑝, each of which is called a character of Γ.
We call 𝑆𝑝the set of character states and, for 𝑠, 𝑡∈𝑆𝑝, define the Hamming distance 𝑑𝐻of 𝑠and 𝑡by 𝑑𝐻(𝑠, 𝑡) = 1 if
𝑠≠𝑡and 𝑑𝐻(𝑠, 𝑡) = 0 otherwise. Then, for a phylogenetic tree (𝑇, 𝜙, 𝑤) of Γ with 𝑇= (𝑉, 𝐸) and an extension 𝐶′ of
𝐶on 𝑉given by a map 𝐶′ ∶𝑉→𝑆𝑝with 𝐶′(𝜙−1(𝑥)) = 𝐶(𝑥) for all 𝑥∈Γ, we call
score(𝑇, 𝐶′) =
∑
(𝑢,𝑣)∈𝐸
𝑑𝐻(𝐶′(𝑢), 𝐶′(𝑣))
the parsimony score of 𝑇and 𝐶. For example, the tree 𝑇= (𝑉, 𝐸) on the left in Figure 1 shows an extension 𝐶′ of the
character 𝐶∶Γ →𝑆2 with 𝐶(𝑥1) = 𝐶(𝑥2) = 0, 𝐶(𝑥4) = 1, 𝐶(𝑥3) = 𝐶(𝑥5) = 𝐶(𝑥6) = 2 on 𝑉with parsimony score
three. A phylogenetic network of Γ is a generalization of a phylogenetic tree (𝑇, 𝜙, 𝑤) to an ordered triplet (𝑁, 𝜙, 𝑤)
where 𝑁is a connected directed acyclic rooted graph with 𝑛vertices of in-degree 1 and out-degree 0. Hereinafter, given
the existence of the bijection 𝜙, with a little abuse of notation, we use the terms phylogenetic tree 𝑇and network 𝑁
(and a same symbol) to indicate both a phylogenetic tree and network and the associated graph, respectively.
To analyse the parsimony score of trees embedded in a phylogenetic network of Γ, Nakhleh et al. [15] introduced

Figure 1: On the left a phylogenetic tree 𝑇with suppressed edge weights. In red, a character 𝐶of Γ and one of its
extensions 𝐶′. score(𝑇, 𝐶′) = 3 is provably minimum among all extensions of 𝐶. In the middle a phylogenetic network 𝑁
such that 𝑇and 𝐶′ constitute a feasible solution to the SPS for 𝑁and 𝐶. On the right a phylogenetic tree ̂𝑇parentally
displayed by 𝑁and an extension 𝐶′ of 𝐶with score( ̂𝑇, 𝐶′) = 2.
Problem 1. Let 𝑁be a phylogenetic network of Γ, let 𝐶be a character of Γ and let be a family of phylogenetic trees
dependent on 𝑁. Then, the Small Parsimony Network Problem (SPN) asks for a tree 𝑇∈such that the parsimony
score of 𝑇and 𝐶is minimum.
The SPN is often called the small parsimony problem in the literature and varies in the definition of its input
graph 𝑁[16]. Hence, we can view the SPN as part of a family of parsimony problems which are constrained to a fixed
input graph and character.
In this article we are interested in finding a most parsimonious tree inside a phylogenetic network 𝑁of Γ with
respect to one character 𝐶of Γ. Such a tree minimizes the parsimony score among all phylogenetic trees "displayed"
by 𝑁. However, a definition of displayed trees depends on our biological model assumptions. For example, Jin et al.
[11] call a phylogenetic tree 𝑇of Γ displayed by a phylogenetic network 𝑁of Γ if 𝑇is isomorphic to a subtree of 𝑁
up to edge subdivisions such that the isomorphism is the identity map on Γ. Under this definition of displayed trees, 𝑇
in Figure 1 is displayed by network 𝑁in the same figure. This means, all descendants of a hybridization vertex 𝑣, i.e.,
a vertex with more than one parent 𝑢in the network, have a shared ancestor 𝑢in the displayed tree. Then, we arrive at
a special case of the SPN [4, 15]:
Problem 2. Let 𝑁be a phylogenetic network of Γ and let 𝐶be a character of Γ. Then, the Softwired Parsimony Score
Problem (SPS) asks for a tree 𝑇displayed by 𝑁such that the parsimony score of 𝑇and 𝐶is minimum.
The limitation of the SPS to displayed trees of a network does exclude evolutionary phenomena like allopoly-
ploidy [22] or incomplete lineage sorting (ILS) [23] which may occur at the time of hybrid speciations. Both events
posit that more than one parent 𝑢of a hybridization vertex 𝑣form common ancestors for descendants of 𝑣. For example,
taxa 𝑥2 and 𝑥3 in the network 𝑁from Figure 1 might inherit genes from ancestors 𝑢1 and 𝑢2, respectively. Hence, the tree
̂𝑇in the same figure is not displayed by 𝑁but might be more parsimonious than any displayed tree of 𝑁with respect
to 𝐶. Indeed, the minimum parsimony score of ̂𝑇and 𝐶in the same figure is smaller than the minimum parsimony
score of a tree displayed by 𝑁. Hence, generalizing the notion of a displayed tree to allow for allopolyploidy or ILS can
aid the development of better phylogenetic reconstruction inference methods [9]. Specifically, we call a phylogenetic
tree 𝑇of Γ parentally displayed by a phylogenetic network 𝑁of Γ if 𝑇is not necessarily isomorphic to a subgraph
of 𝑁but preserves directed paths and degree constraints in 𝑁(up to edge subdivisions) [8]. Then, we obtain another
special case of the SPNP [20]:
Problem 3. Let 𝑁be a phylogenetic network of Γ and let 𝐶be a character of Γ. Then, the Parental Parsimony Score
Problem (PPS) asks for a tree 𝑇parentally displayed by 𝑁such that the parsimony score of 𝑇and 𝐶is minimum.
In general, the complexity of the SPN is determined by the choice of . For example, if contains exactly one
phylogenetic tree, then the SPN can be solved in polynomial time [5]. In contrast, the SPS and PPS are both NP-hard.
Specifically, for binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks [13], the SPS is NP-hard for binary characters [4]. The same
result holds for the PPS even if in addition the network has reticulation depth one [20].
While the change between the SPS and PPS is subtle their combinatorial structure exhibits clear differences already
for small number of taxa. For example, consider the phylogenetic network 𝑁on six taxa in Figure 2. Then, the directed

Figure 2: Consider the set of taxa Γ = {𝑥1, 𝑥2, … , 𝑥6}. Then, the directed graph on the left shows a phylogenetic network
𝑁of Γ. The graph in the middle (right) shows a phylogenetic tree 𝑇𝑆(𝑇𝑃) of Γ which is (parentally) displayed by 𝑁.
path containing edges 𝑒1, 𝑒4 and 𝑒5 is only preserved by the tree 𝑇𝑆and 𝑇𝑃in the same figure among all feasible
solution to the SPS and PPS posed by 𝑁and any character 𝐶of Γ, respectively. The clear difference in the topology
of trees 𝑇𝑆and 𝑇𝑃arises from the three hybridization vertices which are in a shared directed path in 𝑁. To study
the relationship between the SPS and PPS we focus on binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks of Γ. This subclass is
topologically restricted in a specific way which often makes NP-hard optimization problems on phylogenetic networks
(comparatively) easier to solve, see e.g. [6, 10, 21].
In this article, after a preliminary review and study of the SPS, PPS and their connections in Section 2 we extend the
approximation algorithm for the SPS of Frohn and Kelk [6] to an approximation algorithm for the PPS on binary, tree-
child phylogenetic networks in Section 3. In addition, for binary, semi-simplex, tree-child phylogenetic networks, we
show that our method has approximation factor two. Subsequently, in Section 4 we introduce an integer programming
formulation for the PPS on binary phylogenetic networks to design an exact solution algorithm for the PPS. Finally,
in Section 5 we analyze the performance of both our approximate and exact method to solve the PPS on simulated
binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks of Γ and characters of Γ. In Section 5 we conclude this study and discuss
future research directions.
2. Preliminaries
In this section we formalize concepts and introduce notation used throughout the article. For a graph 𝐺we denote
𝑉(𝐺) and 𝐸(𝐺) as the vertex set and edge set of 𝐺, respectively. We call a connected graph 𝐺rooted if 𝐺is directed
and has a unique vertex 𝜌∈𝑉(𝐺) having in-degree zero and there exists a directed path from 𝜌to any vertex of
in-degree 1 and out-degree 0 of 𝐺. We call 𝜌the root of 𝐺and write 𝜌(𝐺) whenever 𝐺is not clear from the context.
In this article we only consider rooted directed acyclic graphs. For 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐺), we denote the induced subgraph of 𝐺
rooted in 𝑣by 𝐺[𝑣]. We call 𝐺[𝑣] pendant if there exists no directed path in 𝐺from a vertex in 𝑉(𝐺[𝑣]) to a vertex
in 𝑉(𝐺) ⧵𝑉(𝐺[𝑣]). We call a directed graph 𝐺binary if, for all 𝑣∈𝑉(𝐺), the in-degree and out-degree of 𝑣sum to
at most three. Moreover, we partition 𝑉(𝐺) into vertices of out-degree zero 𝑉ext(𝐺), called external vertices or leaves,
and 𝑉int(𝐺) = 𝑉(𝐺) ⧵𝑉ext(𝐺), called internal vertices. We omit the mention of 𝐺from the definition of these symbols
whenever 𝐺is clear from the context to simplify our notation. We call hybridization vertices in 𝑉int, i.e., vertices with
in-degree at least two, also reticulation vertices and edges (𝑢, 𝑣) ∈𝐸for which 𝑣is a reticulation vertex reticulation
edges. We call vertices in 𝑉int with in-degree one tree vertices. Any pair of vertices in 𝑉which have a shared parent
vertex are called siblings. When 𝐺is a phylogenetic network, as introduced in the last section, then we assume there
exist no vertices 𝑣∈𝑉with in-degree and out-degree one. Moreover, we call the maximum number of reticulation
vertices on any directed path in 𝐺the reticulation depth of 𝐺. For example, the phylogenetic network 𝑁in Figure 2
has reticulation depth three. We call a phylogenetic network with reticulation depth one also semi-simplex [7]. We call
a rooted phylogenetic network tree-child if at least one child of each vertex in 𝑉int is a tree vertex or leaf. For example,
the network 𝑁in Figure 2 is tree-child. However, if we remove taxon 𝑥4 and the resulting edge subdivision from 𝑁,
then, for edges 𝑒1 = (𝑢1, 𝑣), 𝑒2 = (𝑢2, 𝑣), the unique child of 𝑣is a reticulation vertex, i.e., the network is no longer
tree-child. We denote as the set of rooted, binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks of Γ.

Figure 3: Two possible subgraphs 𝑁1 and 𝑁2 depicting parents and children of a reticulation vertex 𝑟and their
incidence relations in a rooted, binary, tree-child network 𝑁. Bicolored vertices can be internal vertices or leaves. If
the vertices 𝑣𝑟are roots of subtrees 𝑇of 𝑁and 𝑤3, 𝑤4 ∈𝑉(𝑇[𝑣𝑟]) such that 𝑉ext(𝑇[𝑤3]) ∩𝑉ext(𝑇[𝑤4]) ≠∅and
𝑉ext(𝑇[𝑤3]) ∪𝑉ext(𝑇[𝑤4]) = 𝑉ext(𝑇[𝑣𝑟]), then 𝑇1 and 𝑇2 can be extended to trees parentally displayed by 𝑁.
Making a phylogenetic network 𝑁acyclic by deleting exactly one reticulation edge (𝑢, 𝑣) for all reticulation vertices
𝑣is called switching. Let (𝑁) denote the set of all phylogenetic trees displayed by the phylogenetic network 𝑁and
(𝑁) the set of switchings of the network. An attractive property of tree-child networks is that a phylogenetic tree 𝑇
is in (𝑁) if and only if there is a switching 𝑆∈(𝑁) that is isomorphic to 𝑇up to edge subdivisions. This property
holds for tree-child networks 𝑁because in every 𝑆∈(𝑁) and for all 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑆) there exists a path in 𝑆from 𝑣to a
leaf of 𝑁. Hence, in this case it is sufficient to determine a switching of 𝑁to solve the SPS for 𝑁and a given character.
In more general network classes switchings can also be used to characterize displayed trees, but there isomorphism
up to edge subdivisions does not necessarily hold: the switching might contain leaf vertices unlabelled by taxa, for
example.
Since our primary interest in this article is the PPS for binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks and characters
of Γ, we consider a formalization of the PPS that is only valid for binary networks based on the notion of lineage
functions. Van Iersel et al. [20] showed that the PPS for binary phylogenetic networks 𝑁of Γ and a character
𝐶∶Γ →𝑆𝑝can be written as a minimization problem over the class of set functions 𝜆∶𝑉→2𝑆𝑝, called (p-width)
lineage functions on 𝑁. In particular, for a lineage function 𝜆, the authors defined weights 𝑤𝜆(𝑣) for all 𝑣∈𝑉:
𝑤𝜆(𝑣) =

which lead them to prove the following formulation of the PPS:
Proposition 1. Let 𝑁be a rooted, binary phylogenetic network of Γ and let 𝐶∶Γ →𝑆𝑝be a character. Then, the
following formulation of the PPS for 𝑁and 𝐶is valid:
min
{
∑
𝑣∈𝑉(𝑁)
𝑤𝜆(𝑣) ∶𝜆is a 𝑝-width lineage function on 𝑁, 𝜆(𝑥) = {𝐶(𝑥)} ∀𝑥∈Γ, |𝜆(𝜌(𝑁))| = 1
}
.
Notice that Proposition 1 characterizes the objective function value of the PPS but not trees parentally displayed
by 𝑁. In addition, we want to make use of the tree-child property when studying the PPS. To this end, the local structure
of reticulation vertices in a binary, tree-child network is shown in Figure 3 (when allowing 𝑢2 = 𝑣2). Hence, we refer
to the two subnetworks 𝑁1 and 𝑁2 in Figure 3 as the reticulation graphs. Observe that reticulation graphs encode all
possible relationships between adjacent vertices of a reticulation vertex because the tree-child property prohibits pairs
of reticulation vertices from being adjacent or siblings.
Now, we discuss the approximation algorithm for the SPS of Frohn and Kelk [6] summarized in Algorithm 1 as
our first building block to solve the PPS for 𝑁∈and a character 𝐶of Γ. To this end, for a phylogenetic tree 𝑇
and a map 𝐶′ ∶𝑉(𝑇) →2𝑆𝑝, we define the operation resolve(𝑇, 𝐶′) as the traversal of 𝑇[𝑣] in preorder while, for
(𝑎, 𝑏) ∈𝐸(𝑇[𝑣]), setting 𝐶′(𝑏) = 𝐶′(𝑎) if 𝐶′(𝑎) ⊆𝐶′(𝑏), and making 𝐶′(𝑏) a singleton by removing character states at
random otherwise. Furthermore, for an algorithm processing vertices 𝑉int(𝑁), we define a tree-priority queue 𝑄as
a priority queue such that tree vertices have a higher priority than reticulation vertices 𝑟who are ordered based on their

Proposition 2. [6] Let 𝑁∈and let 𝐶be a character of Γ. Then, Algorithm 1 yields a tight 2-approximation

𝐶′(𝑤2) ≠𝐶′(𝑣1), then Algorithm 1 returns an optimal solution.

Figure 5: The graph on the left is a semi-simplex phylogenetic network 𝑁∈. The graph on the right is a reticulation
graph extension 𝑁+ of 𝑁.
time. The graph on the right shows the solution to the SPS returned by Algorithm 1. Clearly, the output of Algorithm 1
depends on the insertion order of vertices added to 𝑄in line 33. Since Proposition 2 applies for any insertion order,
we keep the ordering arbitrary but fixed throughout the article.
While we have seen in Figure 2 that solutions to the SPS and PPS can differ significantly, we will show in the next
section how we can employ Algorithm 1 to approximate the PPS for semi-simplex 𝑁∈with an approximation
factor of two. To this end, we construct auxiliary graphs which map solutions to the SPS for an extended phylogenetic
network of 𝑁∈and a character 𝐶of Γ to solutions to the PPS for 𝑁and 𝐶. Specifically, for reticulation graphs 𝑁1
and 𝑁2 we define their extensions 𝑁+
1 and 𝑁+
2 , respectively, as shown in Figure 6. Then, for semi-simplex 𝑁∈,
we define a reticulation graph extension 𝑁+ of 𝑁as follows:
(i) for reticulation vertices 𝑟∈𝑉(𝑁), consider the pendant subgraph 𝑇of 𝑁with (𝑟, 𝜌(𝑇)) ∈𝐸(𝑁). 𝑇is a subtree
of 𝑁because 𝑁is semi-simplex. Replace 𝑇by two rooted binary subtrees 𝑇1 and 𝑇2 of 𝑇with non-intersecting
leafsets Γ1 and Γ2, respectively, such that Γ1 ∪Γ2 is the leafset of 𝑇, and 𝑇1 and 𝑇2 have the same root as 𝑇(if
it exists).

3. A 2-approximation algorithm for the PPS on binary, semi-simplex, tree-child
phylogenetic networks
In this section we investigate the combinatorial similarities between the PPS and SPS to develop a polynomial
time 2-approximation algorithm for the PPS for semi-simplex 𝑁∈and character 𝐶of Γ. First, we consider binary
characters 𝐶∶𝑉(𝑁) →𝑆1. In this case, Algorithm 2 is applicable and partly resembles Algorithm 1, drawing
parallels between the approximation of the SPS and PPS. However, some crucial steps are different, including a new
resolve procedure: for a rooted connected directed acyclic subgraph 𝑃+ of a reticulation graph extension and a function
𝐶′ ∶𝑉(𝑃+) →2𝑆𝑝, we define the operation resolve_parental(𝑃+, 𝐶′) analogous to resolve(𝑃+, 𝐶′) with the additional
constraints that the preorder traversal does not process edges (𝑎, 𝑏) ∈𝐸(𝑃+) for which 𝑏is a reticulation vertex
of 𝑃+ or 𝑏is a vertex added in part (ii) of the construction of a reticulation graph extension. Moreover, we define
resolve_one_reticulation_graph(𝑃+, 𝐶′) as a relaxed version of operation resolve_parental(𝑃+, 𝐶′) by allowing for
the violation of exactly one of the aforementioned additional constraints in the preorder traversal.
Proposition 3. Let 𝑁∈be semi-simplex and let 𝐶be a binary character of Γ. Then, Algorithm 2 yields a 2-
approximation of the PPS for 𝑁and 𝐶in polynomial time.
Proof. Algorithm 2 constructs a reticulation graph extension 𝑃+ of 𝑁in lines 3 to 5 and subsequently removes
reticulation edges and resulting edge subdivisions until the algorithm terminates. Notice that the initial construction
of 𝑃+ fits into Lemma 1. Hence, after line 5 we have
OPTsoftwired(𝑃+, 𝐶) ≤OPTparental(𝑁, 𝐶).
Lines 7 to 28 are similar to lines 4 to 34 in Algorithm 1 and differ only in two aspects. First, resolve_parental restricts
the resolution of character state sets of procedure resolve in Algorithm 1 such that the preorder traversal terminates
when encountering a reticulation vertex or one of edges (𝑢1, 𝑢+
1 ) or (𝑣1, 𝑣+
1 ) in extension 𝑁+
2 of reticulation graph 𝑁2
(see Figure 6). The continuation of this truncated traversal is detailed in lines 29 to 44 which we will discuss later.
Secondly, lines 20 to 22 in Algorithm 1 are substituted by lines 15 to 18 in Algorithm 2. This means, the parent 𝑢2 of a
reticulation vertex 𝑣is kept as an edge subdivision in 𝑃+ when reticulation edge (𝑢2, 𝑣) is removed only if 𝑢2 is in 𝑁+
2 ,
i.e. (𝑢2, 𝑢+
2 ) ∈𝐸(𝑃+).
After line 28, we conclude that 𝐶′(𝑣) is a non-empty character state (set) for all 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑃+) and 𝐶′ has been
propagated and resolved analogously to Algorithm 1 up to the aforementioned truncation. The modifications of 𝑃+ are
analogous to the modifications of 𝑇in Algorithm 1 except for the lack of removal of edge subdivisions in extensions
𝑁+
2 . Next, lines 29 to 44 remove one reticulation edge incident to each remaining reticulation vertex in 𝑃+ and after
line 45 𝑃+ contains no edge subdivisions, i.e., Algorithm 2 terminates with 𝑃+ as a displayed tree of the reticulation
graph extension constructed in lines 3 to 5. Notice that lines 33 to 40 ensure that 𝑃+ is parentally displayed by 𝑁after
termination by excluding induced subtrees of 𝑁+
2 containing either edges (𝑢1, 𝑣𝑟), (𝑢+
1 , 𝑣+
𝑟) or edges (𝑣1, 𝑣𝑟), (𝑣+

𝑟).
Also, the resolution of 𝐶′ is completed: if a reticulation edge outside of 𝑁+
2 is removed, then line 29 continues the
previously truncated preorder traversal. Otherwise, the removal of one or two reticulation edges in an extension 𝑁+
2
leads to a continuation of the traversal in line 41 truncated at any further occurence of a reticulation vertex or another
extension 𝑁+
2 . The order of if-statements (lines 33, 35 and 39) ensures that the repeat-loop terminates with 𝐶′ being
an extension of 𝐶on 𝑉(𝑃+). Thus, (𝑃+, 𝐶′) is a solution to the PPS for 𝑁and 𝐶after line 45.
Now, we are left to show that the solution (𝑃+, 𝐶′) after line 45 approximates the PPS for 𝑁and 𝐶with a factor
of two. To this end, we consider an induced subtree 𝑇+ of the intermediate solution 𝑃+ at any point of Algorithm 2
such that 𝑇+ is parentally displayed by 𝑁. We prove the following two claims:

The PPS on binary, tree-child networks
Claim 1: the inequality
pp-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤ps-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′)
is an invariant when a reticulation graph or its extension is fully processed by Algorithm 2.
Claim 2: for 𝑁+ = 𝑃+ after line 5, bound
ps-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤2 ⋅OPTsoftwired(𝑁+, 𝐶)
is not obstructed by processing any reticulation graph or its extension.
If claim 2 holds, then after line 45 we get
ps-score(𝑃+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤2 ⋅OPTsoftwired(𝑁+, 𝐶)
from Proposition 2 because lines 29 to 44 in Algorithm 2 are an extension of line 35 in Algorithm 1 (in addition to
processing edge subdivisions) and we have already seen that the modifications of 𝑃+ in Algorithm 2 until line 28
are analogous to the modifications of 𝑇in Algorithm 1 (except for the treatment of edge subdivisions). Thus, using
claims 1 and 2 we can conclude our proof with 𝑃+ after line 45:
pp-score(𝑃+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤ps-score(𝑃+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤2 ⋅OPTsoftwired(𝑁+, 𝐶) ≤2 ⋅OPTparental(𝑁, 𝐶).
(1)
To prove claims 1 and 2, consider a reticulation vertex 𝑣= pop(𝑄) in line 5. Hence, 𝑣induces a reticulation
graph (extension) 𝐺in 𝑃+. By definition of tree-priority queue 𝑄the character state(s) (sets) of the child and at least
one sibling of 𝑣assigned by 𝐶′ are well-defined. In the following case distinction we apply modifications of 𝐺by
Algorithm 2 in the notation of Figures 3 and 6.
Case 1: 𝐺= 𝑁1 (see Figure 3). Then, 𝐶′(𝑤1) and 𝐶′(𝑣𝑟) are propagated to vertex 𝑣2 in 𝑁1. Subsequently, for a partial
solution (𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) such that 𝑇+ is parentally displayed by 𝑁, we have
𝑤𝜆(𝑇+,𝑃+,𝐶′)(𝑟) = |||𝐶′(𝑣𝑟) ⧵(𝐶′(𝑢1) ∪𝐶′(𝑣1))||| ≤min {||𝐶′(𝑣𝑟) ⧵𝐶′(𝑢1)|| , ||𝐶′(𝑣𝑟) ⧵𝐶′(𝑣1)||
}
= min {𝑑𝐻(𝐶′(𝑣𝑟), 𝐶′(𝑢1)), 𝑑𝐻(𝐶′(𝑣𝑟), 𝐶′(𝑣1))} .
(2)
Hence, pp-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤ps-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) is an invariant. Furthermore, Algorithm 1 yields the
same reticulation edge present in 𝐺. Thus, this case does not obstruct bound ps-score(𝑇+, 𝑃+, 𝐶′) ≤2 ⋅
OPTsoftwired(𝑃+, 𝐶) from Proposition 2.
Case 2: 𝐺= 𝑁2 and 𝐺is not an induced subgraph of an extension 𝑁+
2 in 𝑃+.
Case 2.1: 𝐶′(𝑤2) = ∅when 𝑟is processed. Then, 𝐶′(𝑤1) and 𝐶′(𝑣𝑟) are propagated to vertex 𝑢2 in 𝑁2 and no
reticulation edge in 𝐺is removed. Hence, 𝑟remains a reticulation vertex until it is processed in lines 42
and 43. Thus, inequality (2) holds and we can draw the same conclusions as in Case 1.
Case 2.2: 𝐶′(𝑤2) ≠∅when 𝑟is procssed. Then, either no reticulation edge in 𝐺is removed and arguments
analogous to Case 2.1 apply or one reticulation edge in 𝐺is removed and arguments analogous to Case 1
apply.
Case 3: 𝐺= 𝑁+
1 (see Figure 6). Then, exactly one sibling of 𝑟is processed before reticulation vertex 𝑟is processed
and 𝑟is processed before 𝑟+. Hence, analogous arguments to Case 2.1 apply and therefore one reticulation edge
incident to 𝑟is removed in line 43. Then, 𝑃+ induces a tree 𝑇+
1 displayed by 𝐺. Observe that 𝑇+
1 is isomorphic
to tree 𝑇1 in Figure 3. Therefore, by extending 𝑇1 to a subtree 𝑇+ of 𝑃+ which is parentally displayed by 𝑁, we
conclude that
𝑤𝜆(𝑇+,𝑃+,𝐶′)(𝑟) = |||
(𝐶′(𝑤3) ∪𝐶′(𝑤4)) ⧵(𝐶′(𝑢1) ∪𝐶′(𝑣1))|||
≤min {||𝐶′(𝑤3) ⧵𝐶′(𝑢1)|| + ||𝐶′(𝑤4) ⧵𝐶′(𝑣1)|| , ||𝐶′(𝑤4) ⧵𝐶′(𝑢1)|| + ||𝐶′(𝑤3) ⧵𝐶′(𝑣1)||
}
= min {𝑑𝐻(𝐶′(𝑎), 𝐶′(𝑢1)) + 𝑑𝐻(𝐶′(𝑏), 𝐶′(𝑣1)) ∶𝑎, 𝑏∈{𝑤3, 𝑤4}, 𝑎≠𝑏} .

## Page 12


The PPS on binary, tree-child networks
Algorithm 3: exact solution algorithm for the (𝑘, 𝑙)-PPS
Input: Positive integers 𝑘, 𝑙with 𝑘≤𝑙; a set of taxa Γ = {1, … , 𝑛}; a rooted, binary phylogenetic tree 𝑇of Γ; a
𝑘-character 𝐶of Γ
Output: An optimal solution to the (𝑘, 𝑙)-PPS for 𝑇and 𝐶
1 for 𝑣∈𝑉ext(𝑇) do 𝜆(𝑣) ←𝐶(𝑣);
2 for 𝑣∈𝑉int(𝑇) do 𝜆(𝑣) ←∅;
3 Let 𝑄be a queue and add the parents of leaves of 𝑇to 𝑄;
4 do
5
𝑣←pop(𝑄);
6
𝑤1, 𝑤2 ←children of 𝑣(if possible 𝜆(𝑤1) is a set of character state sets);
7
if 𝜆(𝑤1) is a set of character state sets and there exists 𝑐∈𝜆(𝑤1) such that 𝑐∈𝜆(𝑤2) then
8
𝜆(𝑣) ←𝑐;
9
else if 𝜆(𝑤1) and 𝜆(𝑤2) are character state sets and |𝜆(𝑤1) ∪𝜆(𝑤2)| ≤𝑙then
10
𝜆(𝑣) ←𝜆(𝑤1) ∪𝜆(𝑤2);
11
else
12
for 𝑐1 ∈𝜆(𝑤1), 𝑐2 ∈𝜆(𝑤2), 𝑆⊂𝑐1 ∪𝑐2 with |𝑆| = 𝑙and 𝑐1 ∩𝑐2 ⊆𝑆do 𝜆(𝑣) ←𝜆(𝑣) ∪𝑆;
13
if 𝑣= 𝜌(𝑇) then
14
Make 𝜆(𝑣) a character state set by removing elements from 𝜆(𝑣) at random;
15
if 𝜆(𝑣) is a character state set then
16
Set 𝜆(𝑤𝑖) = arg max {|𝜆(𝑣) ∩𝑐| ∶𝑐∈𝜆(𝑤𝑖)}, 𝑖∈{1, 2}, recursively in preorder;

and 𝐶. We formalize this nested subproblem by using the lineage function formulation of the PPS in Proposition 1: for

4. An exact solution algorithm for the PPS on binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks
In this section, we give a branch-&-bound algorithm for the PPS on rooted, binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks
based on a new integer programming formulation of the PPS on rooted, binary networks and an agreement measure
between its LP relaxation and algorithm ApproxPPS. To this end, for a rooted, binary phylogenetic network 𝑁of Γ, a

branching on a non-binary variable value and subsequently choose as the branching variable the one with the largest
improvement in lower bound 𝑊(𝜆𝑎). Strong branching is known to produce small branch-&-bound search trees and
it serves as a useful building block for more sophisticated branching strategies [17]. To measure the improvement by
strong branching, we denote an optimal solution to the LP relaxation of Formulation 1 under the additional constraints
𝑎𝑠
𝑣= 1 for some 𝑣∈𝑉(𝑁), 𝑠∈𝑆𝑝, decoded as a set 𝐹of tuples (𝑣, 𝑠), by (𝑎(𝐹), 𝑐(𝐹)). In addition, we write 𝜆𝐹for
the lineage function returned by ApproxPPS(𝑁.𝐶) under the additional constraints 𝑠∈𝜆(𝑣) for all (𝑣, 𝑠) ∈𝐹. The
latter measures the improvement in upper bounds which can contribute to pruning the search tree further. Since the
disadvantage of strong branching is the high cost of solving many auxiliary linear programs, we combine our strong
branching rule with pseudo-cost branching. Coupled with a capacity constraint on the number of strong branches
this approach is also known as reliability branching [1]. Specifically, we re-use the differences 𝑊(𝜆𝑎(𝐹)) −𝑊(𝜆) we
calculate for strong branching to determine pseudo-cost scores per branching variable based on running averages over
differences 𝑊(𝜆𝑎(𝐹))−𝑊(𝜆). Preliminary computational experiments showed that the LP relaxation of Formulation 1
often contains half-integral values for many variables 𝑎𝑠
𝑣. Hence, we aggregate pseudo-cost scores per vertex 𝑣and do
not differentiate between the different states 𝑠∈𝑆𝑝for branching. Thus, Algorithm 4 is a branch-&-bound algorithm
for the PPS on 𝑁∈adapting both LP lower bounds and upper bounds obtained from ApproxPPS for pruning.

For solutions (𝑎, 𝑐) and 𝜆∗to the LP relaxation and Algorithm 4, respectively, the optimality gap is given by the relative
difference (𝑊(𝜆∗) −𝑊(𝜆𝑎))∕𝑊(𝜆∗). The factor for ApproxPPS is its empirical approximation factor. Branches depicts the
number of coupled strong and pseudo-cost branching decisions (line 28 in Algorithm 4).
5. Computational experiments
In this section we study the performance of the LP relaxation of Formulation 1, algorithm ApproxPPS and
Algorithm 4 on simulated data. Each simulated instance consists of a network 𝑁∈and a character 𝐶of Γ.
Specifically, 𝑁is constructed by first generating a phylogenetic tree of Γ with simulator Ngesh (version 1.2.1) with
default parameters [19], and subsequently inserting edges by sampling pairs of vertices uniformly at random to meet
given requirements on a fixed number of reticulation vertices and a fixed reticulation depth while keeping 𝑁binary
and tree-child. The characters 𝐶are constructed by uniformly at random assigning states from 𝑆𝑝to each taxon in Γ.
All the implementations described have been coded in Python version 3.12.13 by relying on FICO Xpress Optimizer
libraries v47.01.01 for linear programming. All experiments were conducted on a MacBook Pro with M2 chip,
8-core CPU and 10-core GPU, 16 GB RAM and operation system macOS Tahoe (version 26.5.1) using Darwin
kernel (version 25.5.0). The link to the online repository containing all implemented algorithms can be found at
https://github.com/mfrohn/ApproxPPS.
5.1. Empirical results
First, in Table 1 we compare the average quality and runtime of the LP relaxation of Formulation 1, ApproxPPS and
Algorithm 4 on binary, semi-simplex, tree-child networks. Recall that Algorithm 4 employs the other two algorithms
as subroutines both in their original form at the root of the branch-&-bound scheme and adaptively throughout the
branching process. Up to networks with 100 taxa and 40 reticulation vertices the average runtimes do not exceed
two minutes. However, outliers in the data (documented in the online repository) require runtimes of over one hour
individually. This effect is more pronounced for even larger networks which makes the study of average quality and
runtime of Algorithm 4 intractable with an one hour threshold, i.e., 25 hour threshold for each data set. Up to 1000
taxa we observe that the runtime of the LP relaxation scales significantly better than ApproxPPS. This phenomenon
is supported by the simplicity of the constraints in Formulation 1 and its small size as well as the significant
computational overhead of auxiliary calculations in the propagation scheme ApproxPPS to ensure the quality guarantee
of Proposition 5. While an approximation factor of at most two for ApproxPPS is attained for individual inputs, on
average the quality of the approximation is significantly better. Moreover, both the empirical approximation factor
and the optimality gap of the LP relaxation are negatively affected by increases in the number of reticulation vertices.

Figure 8: A boxplot for the runtime of Algorithm 4 on 16 data sets each containing 25 simulated binary, tree-child networks.
The tuples (𝑠, 𝑡) on the x-axis parameterize the networks to have reticulation depth at most 𝑠and number of character
states 𝑡. Furthermore, the figure on the top-left / top-right / bottom-left / bottom-right requires 50/50/100/100 taxa and
5/20/10/40 reticulations, respectively.
In addition, both measures indicate that the underlying methods produce higher quality solutions for more than two
character states. Hence, an average case analysis should align with these observations on data simulated by a birth-death
process with uniformly random edge insertions.
Next, consider the performance of Algorithm 4 illustrated in Figure 8. Observe that plot parameters of the form
(1, 𝑡) correspond to the semi-simplex networks in Table 1. Here, in addition to average runtimes, outliers are shown. We
can see that there are no significant differences between runtimes for fixed numbers of reticulation vertices. Moreover,
no parameter setting is always better than another. However, increasing the reticulation depth to at most five on binary
characters leads to reliably low runtimes for Algorithm 4 with the exception of one outlier for 100 taxa with 10
reticulation vertices. Due to the randomness of the simulated data and the prevalence of statistical outliers in it, further
study is warranted to explain this behaviour. We can also observe that moving from two to four character states slows
Algorithm 4 down in the worst case. This can be explained by the averaging effect in line 28 of Algorithm 4 as pairwise
distinct character states are treated as equally valuable for branching. Hence, extending Algorithm 4 to learn which
character states are more beneficial than others to converge to an optimal solution could ameliorate the runtime increase
for non-binary characters. Finally, comparing the boxplots between the left and right figures in Figure 8 we can see
that the increase in the ratio of reticulation vertices in the respective networks from 10% of leaves to 40% of leaves can
increase the runtime by two magnitudes. This suggests that the complexity of the PPS is more closely linked to the
density of reticulation vertices in the network beyond the reticulation depth measure.
6. Conclusion and future research
We have shown how to approximate the PPS on binary, semi-simplex, tree-child phylogenetic networks and
arbitrary characters with factor two in polynomial time. Furthermore, we have introduced an integer programming
Frohn et al.: Preprint submitted to Elsevier
Page 17 of 19


## Page 18


The PPS on binary, tree-child networks
model for the PPS on binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks to solve the PPS exactly on simulated instances of up
to 100 taxa. To the best of our knowledge this article contains the first empirical study of the PPS. From a practical
point of view this opens up multiple research questions: first, this work does not delve into the biological assumptions
on the input data which could benefit a further structured and larger scale computational study of our new algorithms.
In particular, our algorithms can improve methods like NetRAX [14] who use the parsimony score of a phylogenetic
network as part of an inference of evolutionary histories by extending the support to parental parismony, i.e., allow
for allopolyploidy and incomplete lineage sorting in the inference process. Secondly, our experiments indicate that
the integrality gap of Formulation 1 is relatively small. Moreover, Formulation 1 exhibits structural similarities to the
integer programming model for the SPS by Fischer et al. [4]. While a proof for the integrality gap of both models
is desirable, it also raises the practical question whether the integer programming model for the SPS of Schmidt &
Raphael [18], which shows better performance than the model by Fischer et al. [4] (see Figure 2b in [18]), can be
extended to derive more efficiently tractable lower bounds for the PPS to improve Algorithm 4. Finally, our construction
of Algorithm 4 has hinted at the use of reliability branching. A more detailed study of such machine learning techniques
to solve the PPS exactly would benefit scalability. Specifically, the effects of our adaptive use of lower and upper bounds
to solve the PPS on learned pseudo-costs can be studied to limit redundancies in the branch-&-bound procedure.
Next to the theoretical questions that accompany empirical studies of the PPS we also want to point out some
broader generalizations of the PPS as an operations research problem in itself. On the one hand, the input graph and
vertex labels can be more general than binary, tree-child phylogenetic networks with leaf labels. Both orchard and
tree-based phylogenetic networks [13] generalize the tree-child property, the underlying graph might be considered as
regular instead of binary and labels can be extended to some internal vertices. We have already introduced the latter
point indirectly in our adaptive implementation of ApproxPPS by requiring the input to satisfy 𝑠∈𝜆(𝑣) for some states
𝑠∈𝑆𝑝and any lineage function 𝜆on the corresponding network. Furthermore, the use of the Hamming distance as a
binary/uniform metric to calculate the parental parsimony score can be generalized, too. These variations of the PPS
are reminiscent of the NP-hard metric labeling problem [12] which, given a weighted graph, a set of vertex labels and a
metric distance function asks for an assignment of labels to vertices and the separation of vertices with pairwise distinct
labels, minimizing both an assignment and separation cost. For the uniform metric the metric labeling problem can be
approximated with factor two [12], a version similar to the PPS which excludes the label assignment from the decision
making but in addition asks for an embedding of a most parsimonious parentally displayed tree within the input graph
instead of minimizing the total separation cost of the graph. Studying the connection between the PPS and the metric
labeling problem could therefore unveil structural similarities between problems in phylogenetics and application of
the metric labeling problem such as image segmentation and computer vision.
